Securing smart cities: Unraveling quantum as a service. *Proceedings of the 2nd International Workshop on Quantum Programming for Software Engineering. Presented at the QP4SE ’23: 2nd International Workshop on Quantum Programming for Software Engineering, San Francisco CA USA*. doi:10.1145/3617570.3617865 [→](#9783111342092_020095.xhtml#xref_d31981e33407)
    
    # 12 Methods and tools to improve quantum software quality: a survey
    
    Mahsa Radnejad Khushdeep Kaur Houbing Song Lei Zhang 
    
    **Note:** The first two coauthors have made equal contributions to this chapter.
    
    #### Abstract
    
    With recent breakthroughs in quantum computing, it has become a coming reality instead of a promising future. Quantum computing applications offer revolutionary potential across multiple domains including artificial intelligence (AI), optimization, healthcare, energy, and space, known as quantum advantage.
    
    The power of quantum computing relies on novel quantum algorithms, quantum software, and hardware. Unlike classical software, quantum software has unique features because of quantum mechanics such as superposition and noncloning. This opens a new research field – quantum software engineering (QSE). While the software engineering (SE) research community became aware of this need in 2019, we noticed the lack of a comprehensive investigation of state-of-the-art technologies and tools to improve quantum software quality. Testing and debugging are the two most efficient approaches to assure software quality in classical SE. In QSE, testing and debugging quantum programs become challenging due to quantum mechanics. While we can leverage some best practices from the classical world, new techniques and tools are needed to address the concerns in QSE.
    
    In this chapter, we first conduct a survey study of the state-of-the-art technologies and tools for testing and debugging quantum software. This study includes but is not limited to quantum bug pattern analysis and detection, quantum software testing techniques and classification, and quantum debugging techniques. In the second place, we provide our visions and insights of testing and debugging quantum software in terms of challenges and opportunities for improving quantum software quality. This survey has the potential to foster a research community committed to developing novel methods and tools for QSE.
    
    Keywords:  Quantum computing,  quantum software engineering,  quantum software testing,  quantum software debugging,  
    
    ## 12.1 Introduction
    
    Quantum computing utilizes quantum mechanisms to process quantum information, which can be exponentially faster than what is possible with classical computers (CCs). The supremacy of quantum computers (QCs) over CCs is called “quantum advantage.” The potential of quantum computing is grounded in the properties of quantum physics including but not limited to superposition, wavefunction collapse, entanglement, and no-cloning \[[1](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_001)\].
    
    In 1981, Dr. Richard Feyman discussed using a QC to simulate quantum systems \[[2](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_002)\]. In 1985, Deutsch’s Quantum Turing Machine, which formalizes the concept of using QCs to perform quantum computations, was introduced by Dr. David Deutsch \[[3](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_003)\]. However, it was not until 1998 that the first QC was created by the Los Alamos National Laboratory, the Massachusetts Institute of Technology, and the University of California at Berkeley with only two qubits (quantum bits \[[4](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_004)\]). In the last decade, quantum hardware has grown dramatically – the current qubit record holder is IBM’s Osprey computer, with 433 qubits (but IBM has not yet released details about how it performs until this chapter is completed).
    
    Like CCs, QCs have both hardware and software. Meanwhile, the hardware of QCs is being developed rapidly. On the software side, quantum programs are mostly on the lower lever, that is, we use assembly programming languages to manipulate qubits. Existing quantum software development platforms and programming languages include but are not limited to IBM Qiskit (based on OpenQASM \[[5](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_005)\]), Microsoft Quantum Development Kit (Q#, \[[6](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_006)\]), Tensorflow Quantum \[[7](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_007)\], Cirq \[[8](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_008)\], Scaffold \[[9](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_009)\], and Silq (considered to be a high-level programming language for quantum computing \[[10](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_010)\]). The development of quantum software is harnessed by software engineering (SE) principles. Quantum software engineering (QSE) plays a significant role in developing quantum software systems \[[11](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_011)\]. QSE covers the whole quantum software development life cycle including requirements engineering, system modeling, implementation, testing, maintenance, and evolution.
    
    While most quantum programming languages are at lower levels, QSE methods and tools are similar to what happened in the classical world back in the 1960s and 1970s. At that time, the waterfall model was just introduced. However, the field lacked efficient methods and tools to maintain the quality of the software and the speed of software development. The history is repeating itself in QSE. Due to quantum mechanics, it is extremely challenging to test and debug quantum programs. For example, the indeterminacy of quantum computing produces random results. Thus, testing a quantum program also generates indeterminate test results, which may pass or fail randomly. This leads to flaky tests, which should be avoided during software development \[[12](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_012)\].
    
    The increasing size and complexity of quantum programs have led to a rise in bugs within quantum source code, paralleling similar challenges in classical software development. While classical SE has a rich array of tools and methodologies for bug detection and elimination \[[13](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_013), [14](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_014)\], quantum software development demands novel approaches to enhance quality. Existing research explores adapting classical practices to quantum such as search-based testing techniques \[[15](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_015), [16](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_016), [17](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_017)\]. Notable contributions include QBugs \[[18](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_018)\], which introduces reproducible bugs in quantum algorithms, fostering controlled quantum software testing and debugging experiments. Tailored approaches have emerged, encompassing fuzz testing \[[19](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_019), [20](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_020), [21](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_021)\] and mutation testing \[[22](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_022), [23](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_023), [24](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_024)\]. Novel tools like QuanFuzz \[[20](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_020)\], QMutPy \[[23](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_023)\], and Muskit \[[24](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_024)\] have been proposed. Researchers delve into bug pattern identification \[[25](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_025)\], comprehensive bug fix studies \[[26](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_026)\], and static analysis \[[27](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_027)\]. Additionally, classical debugging elements like assertions have been adapted to the quantum landscape, with runtime, statistical, and dynamic assertions applied to quantum circuits \[[28](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_028), [29](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_029), [30](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_030), [31](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_031)\]. The evolving field reflects a concerted effort to develop specialized methodologies, tools, and techniques for testing and debugging quantum software, ensuring the reliability and efficiency of quantum computing technology.
    
    We have two **contributions** in this chapter. First, we present a comprehensive survey of testing and debugging techniques in QSE. We summarize existing methods and tools for quantum bug pattern detection, bug-fix pattern detection, bug benchmarking, testing, and debugging. We categorize bug pattern detection and testing techniques.
    
    Second, we also provide thorough insights about how to improve the quality of quantum software, that is, the challenges, research directions, and opportunities. We hope this survey will help both researchers and practitioners prepare the knowledge foundations of QSE and foster novel research ideas in QSE.
    
    The chapter is structured as follows. Section 12.2 introduces the bug patterns, bug pattern detection techniques, and benchmarks in QSE. Section 12.3 describes testing methods, tools, and classification for quantum programs. In Section 12.4, we review debugging tactics and put them into three categories. In Section 12.5, we discuss research challenges and opportunities for quantum software testing and debugging. Then, we conclude the chapter in Section12.6.
    
    ## 12.2 Quantum bugs
    
    In this section, we will introduce quantum software bug patterns, bug pattern detection tools, and quantum bug benchmarks, respectively.
    
    ### 12.2.1 Bug patterns
    
    A software bug is formally defined as an aberrant behavior within a program characterized by an error, flaw, or fault in the design, development, or operation of the software, causing it to deviate from its originally intended functionality \[[32](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_032)\]. To address this issue, various SE activities, including program analysis, debugging, and software testing, can be performed, focusing on developing techniques and tools for detecting and resolving errors.
    
    Software bugs are from various sources. Syntax bugs are associated with incorrect characters in source code, and semantic bugs occur when there are no compiling errors, but the program does not perform the correct logic. A comprehensive understanding of the behaviors associated with quantum program bugs is imperative in debugging and testing quantum software. On the other hand, just as bugs exist in classical testing and detection programs, they are equally prevalent within quantum programs. The significance of this issue is poised to increase with the progression of quantum programming languages, such as Q# and Cirq, all of which play a pivotal role in shaping the quantum software landscape.
    
    In recent years, investigations into bugs and debugging in quantum software have contributed to heightened awareness among researchers regarding the existence of bugs and the need for their subsequent mitigation in quantum programming.
    
    Huang and Martonosi \[[28](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_028), [33](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_033)\] propose a range of bugs and checked defenses against these bugs in quantum programs. They recognized this as a critical issue against the increasing progress of quantum computing. Their study focuses on the types of bugs in specific quantum programs to support the debugging of quantum software. According to the experience gained, they created a classification of bugs in quantum computing.
    
    Zhao et al. \[[25](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_025)\] present some bug patterns in the Qiskit framework, along with their classification. They offer a comprehensive exploration of QSE in general, highlighting various tools, approaches, and unresolved issues in the field.
    
    Paltenghi and Pradel \[[34](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_034)\] focus on the bugs on quantum computing platforms and present an empirical study of real-world bugs in open-source quantum programs. Their research encompasses an exhaustive statistical analysis of bugs and introduces several pertinent bug patterns within quantum computing platforms.
    
    Luo et al. \[[26](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_026)\] extend some new bugs to improve the procedures based on classification used in the work of Paltenghi and Pradel \[[34](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_034)\] in quantum programs. They gathered 223 real-world bug cases and their solutions from various quantum programming languages and development platforms such as Qiskit, Q#, and Cirq. These bugs were categorized into four primary groups: application programming interface (API)-related, incorrect application logic, math-related, and others, each with its subcategories. This categorization offers a detailed understanding of quantum program bugs, aiding in the testing and debugging of such programs. Their study is noted as a pioneering effort in addressing bugs in quantum software.
    
    [Table 12.1](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_tab_001) presents a comprehensive list of bug patterns in quantum programs, detailing each section with cause/descriptions, categories, bug patterns, and references. This compilation was inspired by Zhao et al. \[[25](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_025)\], who provided a list of bug patterns; we expanded upon this by incorporating additional patterns from recent articles and research \[[26](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_026), [34](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_034), [35](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_035)\]. The table delineates five main categories, that is, (1) initialization, (2) gate operation, (3) measurement, (4) logic, and (5) API. Note that the list of quantum bug categories in [Table 12.1](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_tab_001) is not exhaustive, but a highlight of common quantum bug patterns.
    
    Table 12.1:Quantum bug patterns.
    
     
    | Category  || Bug pattern || Cause/description |
    | --- | --- | --- |
    | Initialization || Unequal classical bits and qubits || Classical bits are smaller in magnitude than qubits used for operations or measurements \[[25](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_025)\]. |
    | --- | --- | --- |
    | Insufficient initial qubits || A quantum circuit should be initialized with enough qubits; otherwise, it may cause errors in the quantum circuit \[[25](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_025)\]. |
    | Inappropriate modification of register size || Changing the register size can modify its hash value and bits, making it unsuitable as a key in structures like sets \[[25](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_025)\]. |
    | Gate operation || Custom gates are not recognized || Custom gates may violate the nature of quantum mechanics \[[25](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_025)\]. |
    | Measurement || Over repeated measurement || Iterations of measurements can exceed the capacity and cause unintended consequences \[[25](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_025)\]. |
    | Incorrect operations after measurement || Continual changes to measured qubits including state changes and reentanglement with other qubits \[[25](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_025)\]. |
    | Method measure\_all || Developers may not know that measure\_all autogenerates classical registers for storing all measured qubits \[[25](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_025)\]. |
    | Logic || Incorrect application logic || Errors in the application’s logic are often unique to quantum computing platforms (subcategories can also be found in \[[26](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_026), [34](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_034)\]). |
    | API || Misuse of methods or formula || Errors from improper method usage like incorrect arguments, misspelled names, or wrong mathematical models in code \[[26](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_026), [34](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_034)\]. |
    
    First, *initialization* in quantum computing is a critical phase where classical bits and qubits are prepared for subsequent operations. The bug patterns in this category highlight issues such as (1) unequal magnitudes between classical bits and qubits during initialization \[[25](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_025)\], (2) the potential inadequacy of initial qubits in specific methods \[[25](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_025)\], and (3) the risks associated with inappropriate modifications to register size \[[25](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_025)\]. These patterns underscore the importance of precision and consistency in setting up the foundational elements of quantum programs. “Unequal classical bits and qubits” is a bug pattern in this category that occurs when the number of classical bits is less than the number of qubits, which complicates the storage of measurement results. Moreover, “insufficient initial qubits” occur in certain parametric circuits like the TwoLocal method, where there are not enough qubits for the needs of the circuit. In addition, the bug pattern “inappropriate modification of register size” in quantum programming occurs when changing a register’s size leads to errors, especially in complex circuits, by altering the hash values of the register and its bits, thus affecting its usage in data structures like sets.
    
    Second, in quantum computing, the effective operation of custom gates is paramount for the accuracy of quantum programs. The bug pattern “custom gates are not recognized” highlights challenges arising when attempting to create controlling gates larger than three qubits, violating the principle of two-qubit entanglement \[[25](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_025)\]. Recognizing custom gates is crucial for the proper execution of quantum algorithms, and deviations from entanglement principles can lead to recognition issues, impacting the reliability of quantum computations. Addressing this bug pattern is essential for ensuring the precise functioning of quantum circuits and maintaining the integrity of quantum algorithms.
    
    Third, *measurement* serves as a pivotal process, extracting crucial information from quantum systems. The bug pattern “over repeated measurement” \[[25](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_025)\] highlights the significance of judiciously managing the quantity of measurement iterations to prevent unintended consequences, emphasizing the delicate balance needed in this critical phase. “Incorrect operations after measurement” \[[25](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_025)\] delves into the nuanced complexities of postmeasurement modifications to qubits, encompassing state alterations and re-entanglement, shedding light on the potential challenges in maintaining the integrity of quantum states. The introduction of the method measure\_all adds an extra layer of insight, revealing its automatic generation of a classical register to store all measured qubits \[[25](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_025)\], providing developers with a valuable awareness of the automatic processes that might influence their quantum programs.
    
    Fourth, the correct *application logic* is paramount for the success of any computing paradigm, and quantum computing is no exception. This category focuses on bug patterns stemming from mistakes within the application’s logic \[[26](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_026), [34](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_034)\]. Unlike issues encountered in classical computing, the identified bugs in this category are distinctly tailored to the intricacies of quantum computing platforms. This distinctiveness arises from the unique principles of quantum mechanics and the specialized requirements of quantum algorithms. Identifying and addressing these logic-related issues becomes imperative for developers aspiring to craft resilient and error-free quantum programs.
    
    Last but not least, *API* interactions play a vital role in facilitating the effective utilization of quantum resources. Bug patterns in this category are associated with the misuse of methods or formulas, representing instances where developers may inadvertently deviate from the intended use of specific functions. The bug pattern “misuse of methods or formula” sheds light on errors such as employing an incorrect number of arguments, misspelling method names, or applying inaccurate mathematical formulas or models in the quantum code \[[26](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_026), [34](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_034)\]. The API category serves as a guide for developers to navigate these potential pitfalls, ensuring the proper integration of quantum methods and formulas to enable the seamless execution of quantum algorithms.
    
    ### 12.2.2 Bug pattern detection techniques
    
    Bug pattern detection, in the realm of software development, entails the systematic procedure of recognizing and categorizing errors or flaws automatically within software programs. In the context of quantum programming, the focal objective of bug pattern detection is to discern and resolve programming challenges distinct from quantum computing.
    
    The research of quantum software bug pattern detection is critical because analyzing quantum bug patterns helps developers understand the uniqueness of quantum bugs and develop tools for fix prediction. In the last few years, there have been several research studies about static bug detectors in quantum programs. QChecker \[[27](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_027)\] is a specialized static analysis tool meticulously crafted for quantum programs developed in Qiskit. It encompasses modules that extract program data through abstract syntax trees (ASTs) and identify bugs by applying pattern-based detection methods.
    
    LintQ \[[36](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_036)\] is a static analysis framework designed to detect bugs and patterns in quantum programs by using abstractions that reason about common concepts in quantum computing without relying on the details of the underlying quantum computing platform. The authors evaluate the performance of LintQ and show that it has a precision of 80.5% when detecting quantum software errors. Our previous study pushes the research of bug pattern detection even further by proposing an automated bug-fix pattern detection tool \[[37](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_037)\]. We leverage ASTs and regular expressions to check the syntax and semantics of bug-fix pairs and detect patterns in terms of initialization, operations, and measurements.
    
    ### 12.2.3 Quantum bug benchmark
    
    Quantum bug benchmarks can be used to evaluate the effectiveness of bug pattern detection techniques and debugging approaches. Benchmarks can also provide quantitative comparisons among different bug detection and debugging techniques.
    
    Campos and Souto \[[18](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_018)\] introduce a benchmark named QBugs for quantum software systems such as ProjectQ and Qiskit. QBugs provides a collection of reproducible bugs, and it introduces essential concepts for establishing a benchmark, serving as a foundational component for building an experimental framework. This framework aids in evaluating and comparing new research efforts while ensuring the reproducibility of prior research outcomes in the realm of QSE. Moreover, the work also addresses the challenges and potential future directions related to the development of QBugs.
    
    Zhao et al. \[[35](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_035)\] introduce Bugs4Q, a novel benchmark comprising 36 real-world bugs extracted from four IBM Qiskit programs (i.e., Terra, Aer, Ignis, and Aqua). The main purpose of Bugs4Q is to facilitate quantum software testing and debugging of quantum software by providing real-world test cases to identify and address buggy behaviors. It aggregates and categorizes reproducible Qiskit program bugs, making them publicly available for research purposes. Moreover, Zhao et al. \[[38](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_038)\] expanded on their work by enhancing Bugs4Q, which now encompasses 42 manually validated real bugs from Qiskit programming, along with corresponding test cases to replicate the faulty behaviors. This suite serves as a user-friendly platform for executing Qiskit programs and grants access to both the buggy and stable versions, enabling reproducible research and benchmarking of Qiskit debugging and testing tools.
    
    ## 12.3 Quantum software testing
    
    The need for quantum software testing has garnered considerable attention, especially since the first publication of QSE at the international conference on SE in 2019 \[[2](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_002)\]. Quantum software testing focuses on detecting faults in quantum programs and cost-effectively identifying quantum errors \[[39](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_039)\]. Quantum programmers may encounter challenges when testing quantum software due to quantum mechanics such as superposition, entanglement, and no-cloning. For example, a single testing run cannot describe the whole probability distribution because of the random nature of quantum computing \[[29](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_029)\]. However, there exist similarities between QSE and classical SE (especially classical software that produces probability distributions). Miranskyy and Zhang \[[2](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_002)\] discuss the possibilities to leverage some classical SE best practices to quantum software development, focusing on testing activities, such as white box and black box testing.
    
    In this section, we will explore the advancements in quantum software testing methods, across four categories, that is, white box testing, black box testing, dynamic testing, and static testing.
    
    *   **White box testing****:** White box testing is a method to test the application at the source code level. It offers the advantage of enabling a more profound analysis of the predicates delineating each model operation, thereby potentially enabling the generation of tests of greater sophistication. As discussed by Miranskyy et al. \[[40](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_040)\], white box testing techniques are not limited to traditional software but can also be effectively employed in QC simulators. This approach involves a meticulous examination of the internal code listings, enabling the execution of standard white box activities, such as code reviews and inspections, even within the context of quantum computing.
        
    *   **Black box testing****:** Black box testing is a method of testing approach applicable across all levels of testing and entails the evaluation of software functionality without delving into its internal composition or mechanisms. Testers utilizing this method concentrate exclusively on the software’s input and output behavior, devoid of knowledge regarding its underlying implementation, aiming to ascertain its adherence to specified requirements and expected performance. Black box testing in the context of quantum computing refers to the testing approach where the internal workings of a quantum program running on an actual QC cannot be observed directly, and the program is treated as a black box. This is in contrast to white box testing, which can be performed on QC simulators where the internal code listing can be examined \[[40](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_040)\].
        
    *   **Dynamic testing****:** Dynamic testing is a fundamental approach within software testing, characterized by the live execution of software code to assess its real-time behavior and performance. This methodology offers several advantages, such as its proficiency in revealing elusive errors and defects, contributing significantly to overall testing quality.
        
    *   Moreover, dynamic testing, by subjecting the software to execution in a true-to-life operational context, provides an authentic evaluation of its performance and functionality, delivering valuable insights into real-world operation. However, due to the nature of real-time execution, dynamic testing can be time-intensive for QSE, particularly when dealing with a multitude of test cases, which is common in quantum programs because of probability distributions in quantum mechanics.
        
    *   **Static testing****:** Static testing, a well-established discipline in SE, involves the meticulous analysis of source code to preemptively identify potential defects, all without the need for code execution. Employing specialized code analysis tools, static testing inspects the codebase, flagging potential issues for further investigation and resolution. A key advantage is its capacity to operate before the existence of an executable software version, efficiently detecting insidious faults like memory leaks. This approach also excels in fault localization, as it tends to expose symptoms near their root causes. Utilizing compilers or parsers aligned with the project’s programming language, static testing systematically constructs a comprehensive representation of the codebase, facilitating the identification of potential issues and enhancing software quality and reliability.
        
    *   Numerous categories of classical software testing approaches have been proposed, whereas only a limited selection is currently available for quantum software. In this section, we highlight some techniques for testing quantum programs, which are described and categorized in [Table 12.2](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_tab_002).
        
    *   **Search-based testing****:** The search-based testing approach is not limited to evaluating classical software programs but is also increasingly being employed in quantum software \[[48](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_048)\]. The search-based method in testing quantum programs is a multifaceted approach that leverages optimization techniques, such as genetic algorithms (GAs) or random search \[[48](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_048)\], to automate the creation of test cases (test case generation), strategically arrange them in order of importance (test case prioritization), and reduce the test suite to its most efficient form (test suite minimization).
        
    
    Table 12.2:Quantum software testing techniques, categories, and tools.
    
    | Techniques || Categories || Tools |
    | --- | --- | --- |
    | Search-based testing || White/black box and dynamic testing || QuSBT \[[16](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_016)\]  
    MutTG \[[17](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_017)\] |
    | --- | --- | --- |
    | Mutation testing || White box and dynamic testing || Muskit \[24\]  
    QMutPy \[[23](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_023), [41](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_041)\] |
    | Property-based testing || Black box and dynamic testing || QSharpCheck \[[42](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_042)\] |
    | Metamorphic testing || Black box and dynamic testing || MorphQ \[[43](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_043)\] |
    | Fuzz testing || Black box and dynamic testing || QuanFuzz \[[20](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_020)\] |
    | Combinatorial testing || Black box and dynamic testing || QuCAT \[[44](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_044), [45](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_045)\] |
    | Multiple subroutines || White/black box and dynamic/static testing || QSharpTester \[[46](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_046)\] |
    | Quantum symbolic execution || White box and static testing || symQV \[[47](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_047)\] |
    | AST-based static analysis || White box and static testing || QChecker \[[27](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_027)\] |
    
    In the realm of search-based testing in QSE, Wang et al. \[[16](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_016)\] have proposed a tool called QuSBT, which employs GA to enhance testing effectiveness by maximizing the number of failing test cases in a test suite. QuSBT focuses on balancing the quality and cost of test inputs by using GA to find optimal test inputs that optimize quality. The authors validate QuSBT on IBM Qiskit framework. The experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of QuSBT in detecting failures in quantum programs. Similar to QuSBT, Wang et al. \[[17](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_017)\] introduce MutTG, which is also a search-based approach aimed at optimizing test generation for quantum programs. By creating a minimal test suite, MutTG aims to improve reliability by efficiently identifying and addressing program mutants.
    
    *   **Mutation testing:** Mutation testing is another widely used software testing technique to assess the effectiveness of test cases by introducing artificial faults, known as “mutants,” into the code. It falls into the category of white box testing, wherein testers purposefully modify the source code to assess how well the software test suite can detect these alterations. The main objective of these modifications is to introduce defects into the software intentionally. Additionally, mutation testing serves as a popular approach for creating new tests involving small changes to a program that results in a new version of the software. In the realm of mutation testing, a substantial body of research has been developed, leading to notable contributions in the field. Collectively, these valuable insights and advancements collectively offer a comprehensive overview of the knowledge available on mutation testing and the tools used for this purpose.
        
    *   One of these tools is Muskit \[[24](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_024)\], a mutation analysis tool designed for quantum programs coded in IBM’s Qiskit framework. It defines mutation operators on the gates of QPs and provides selection criteria to reduce the number of mutants generated. Muskit facilitates the execution of test cases on mutants and generates results for test analyses. It is accessible as a command line interface, GUI, and web application. Muskit comprises two primary components: Mutants Generator and Mutants Executor, in addition to Test Analyzer, which the users can implement as per their specific analysis requirements. The tool has been validated to generate and execute mutants for four QPs. Currently, Muskit cannot detect equivalent mutants, but the authors plan to implement techniques to detect them as more knowledge becomes available. Another notable tool, QMutPy \[[23](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_023), [41](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_041)\], is an extension of the open-source mutation testing tool MutPy \[[49](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_049)\], specifically designed for quantum programs. QMutPy tool introduces a novel set of mutation operators based on qubit measurements and quantum gates to generate mutants for testing quantum programs. QMutPy has been evaluated through a case study involving 24 real quantum programs written in IBM’s Qiskit library, demonstrating its effectiveness in enhancing test suite coverage and mutation score.
        
    *   **Property-based testing:** Property-based testing, as a testing methodology, relies on the definition of essential properties to establish criteria and procedures for testing, with a particular focus on these properties \[[50](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_050)\]. In the context of QSE, Honarvar et al. \[[42](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_042)\] propose QSharpCheck, which is a property-based testing framework designed for Microsoft Q#. QSharpCheck is also equipped with five assertions, for example, “AssertProbability” and “AssertEntangled.” The authors evaluate the effectiveness of QSharpCheck via mutation analysis, and results show that QSharpCheck achieved 80% and 60% of mutation scores for two examples, respectively.
        
    *   **Metamorphic testing****:** Metamorphic testing \[[51](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_051), [43](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_043)\] refers to a software testing approach that focuses on creating test cases and validating test results by using metamorphic relationships, which specify the relationship between an output of a program and an input (which undergoes certain transformations). Abreu et al. \[[51](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_051)\] present metamorphic testing in oracle quantum programs. Their methodology relies on metamorphic relations, effectively resolving the well-known quantum measurement problem (i.e., wave function collapse). The authors validate their approach with IBM Qiskit, and the results show that, by using quantum search algorithms (such as Grover’s algorithm), their approach can speed up the testing process (improving the time complexity from *O*(*N*) to *O*(*√N*)). Another contribution of metamorphic testing is called MorphQ, proposed by Paltenghi and Pradel \[[43](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_043)\]. MorphQ consists of two components, that is, (1) a program generator that creates a diverse range of valid quantum programs and (2) a set of program transformations that leverage quantum-specific metamorphic relationships to alleviate the oracle problem. MorphQ is evaluated via Qiskit. In the experiments, it creates over 8,000 program pairs within 2 days, which exposes many crashes. Then, 13 bugs are found from those crashes.
        
    *   **Fuzz testing****:** Fuzz testing (also known as fuzzing) involves injecting unexpected inputs or random data (known as fuzz) into the software to assess its behaviors. In QSE, De las Cuevas et al. \[[52](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_052)\] propose QuanFuzz, which uses a matrix generator to generate test cases with higher coverage. QuanFuzz is validated over various quantum circuits to trigger sensitive branches for coverage testing. The testing results show that QuanFuzz achieves 20–60% more coverage compared to traditional testing input generation on quantum programs.
        
    *   **Combinatorial testing****:** Combinatorial testing is a testing technique in which multiple combinations of input parameters are used to perform testing \[[53](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_053)\]. Wang et al. \[[45](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_045)\] propose a combinatorial testing tool called QuCAT, which provides two functionalities for generating test suits. The first functionality generates a test suite of a given strength, and the second functionality generates test suites with increasing strength. The authors assess the efficiency and effectiveness of QuCAT via five quantum programs. Their results show that QuCAT with a low strength can find bugs efficiently, and QuCAT with a higher strength can perform better (finding more complicated bugs) with a higher cost.
        
    *   **Multiple subroutines****:** Multiple subroutine testing techniques focus on quantum programs with multisubroutines. For example, complex quantum programs can be divided into small subroutines, improving code reusability and maintainability. In 2022, Long and Zhao \[[46](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_046)\] propose QSharpTester, which is a tool for testing multi-subroutine quantum programs (in Q#). QSharpTester includes three test coverage criteria, that is, classical-superposition partition, classical-superposition-mixed partition, and entanglement partition for partitioning quantum programs. The authors use hundreds of mutation quantum programs derived from seven Q# programs to validate the effectiveness of QSharpTester. The experimental results show that QSharpTester performs well on a variety of quantum bugs in Q# on those mutation programs.
        
    *   **Quantum symbolic execution****:** Quantum symbolic execution proposed by Nan et al. \[[54](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_054)\] is a novel testing approach for generating high-coverage test cases to debug quantum programs. Quantum symbolic execution differs from classical symbolic execution by using quantum superposition and parallelism to store and continually divide the test case space into subsets based on conditional statements in the program, thereby enabling the identification of appropriate test cases for different code branches with reduced complexity. Moreover, the symbolic quantum programming \[[55](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_055)\] approach emphasizes the development of tools for symbolic manipulation of quantum circuits and testing quantum programs. It aims to support parallel and distributed computing and involves basic structures for local manipulation of qubits. Additionally, a new framework proposed by Bauer-Marquart et al. \[[47](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_047)\], symQV is a framework for writing and verifying quantum programs in the quantum circuit model, aiming for automated verification of quantum programs executed symbolically.
        
    *   **AST-based static analysis****:** As discussed in Quantum bugs section, QChecker \[[27](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_027)\] is a static analysis tool for detecting bugs of quantum programs developed in Qiskit. It encompasses modules that extract program data through ASTs and identify bugs by applying pattern-based detection methods. To the best of our knowledge, it is the only static analysis tool for testing and bug detection in quantum software.
        
    
    ### 12.3.1 Noise in quantum computing
    
    Quantum noise presents a formidable challenge within the domain of quantum computing, posing a significant obstacle to achieving the desired levels of computational accuracy. In contrast to CCs, where outcomes are deterministically predictable, quantum programs are susceptible to the disruptive effects of quantum noise, resulting in unanticipated errors and deviations from anticipated results \[[56](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_056)\]. Thus, understanding and modeling quantum noise is crucial for software developers transitioning from classical programming to quantum programming.
    
    Pan et al. \[[57](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_057)\] worked on the first study to explore quantum noise from a SE perspective. In another work, Resch and Karpuzcu \[[58](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_058)\] attempted to present various causes of quantum noise. In addition, Martina et al. \[[59](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_059)\] identified machine learning to detect computer noise in the hardware of noisy intermediate scale QCs because each computer has a noise footprint that can be learned about. However, a new approach presented by Muqeet et al. \[[60](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_060)\] can reduce noise’s effect on the test results of a quantum program called QOIN.
    
    ## 12.4 Quantum software debugging
    
    Debugging, an essential part of software development, takes a whole new dimension in the quantum realm. QCs operate on the principles of quantum physics, a field known for its counterintuitive and amusing properties. Particles exist in superposition in this quantum universe, capable of representing many values at the same time. In the quantum world, measurement, which is a crucial debugging step in classical computing, takes on a dual purpose. While in superposition, quantum bits exist in an indeterminate state. However, when measured, this quantum superposition collapses, potentially resulting in unintentional errors and rendering typical debugging approaches ineffective.
    
    Debugging quantum software confronts the Heisenberg uncertainty principle \[[61](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_061)\], which underscores the challenges of quantum measurement. In classical computing, measurements reveal the state of a variable without altering it. In quantum computing, measurement disrupts the state of a qubit, introducing quantum uncertainty into the debugging process. Understanding how measurements affect the quantum state is essential for effective debugging \[[62](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_062)\].
    
    ### 12.4.1 Debugging tactics in classical SE
    
    Miranskyy et al. \[[40](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_040)\] categorize debugging tactics into three groups, that is, (1) backtracking, (2) cause elimination, and (3) brute force, each with its strengths and challenges. These three tactics apply in both the classical and quantum SE:
    
    *   **Backtracking tactics:** Backtracking tactics use conventional debugging techniques to trace quantum bugs systematically \[[40](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_040)\]. Using the quantum stack trace, developers may trace bugs back to their origin, providing significant insights into the quantum error propagation mechanism. This method helps in locating the specific line of code containing the incorrect code, which speeds up the debugging process. It should be emphasized, however, that quantum stack traces can be lengthy, especially in complex quantum algorithms, and that prolonged retracing may be resource-intensive, potentially slowing down debugging procedures.
        
    *   **Cause elimination tactics****:** Cause elimination tactics provide an effective strategy for resolving bugs in quantum software. This approach involves systematically ruling out potential causes of bugs in quantum programs, gradually narrowing down to the root cause \[[40](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_040)\]. Developers can make this tactic a valuable debugging strategy when they use repetitive cause elimination tactics. For this reason, this tactic would be time-consuming due to the nature of the process. On the other hand, this approach is remarkably efficacious when dealing with complex bugs. Depending on the complexity of the program, cause elimination strategies may be resource-intensive as well.
        
    *   **Brute force tactics****:** This methodology comprises thorough testing and analysis of quantum states in order to detect anomalies and bugs. Developers can find errors that may not be seen using conventional debugging methods by carefully investigating the whole range of parameters and quantum state shifts, providing excellent precision in spotting bugs \[[40](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_040)\]. However, it is essential to note that brute force approaches are resource-intensive, costing a significant amount of processing power and time. The computational cost associated with brute force debugging in large-scale quantum systems may make it impracticable.
        
    
    ### 12.4.2 Debugging tactics in QSE
    
    Several debugging techniques have been proposed and applied in QSE. The most popular technique is to utilize assertions in debugging. Some quantum platforms, like IBM quantum platform \[[63](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_063)\], also provide interactive debugging services. We will discuss these techniques in detail below.
    
    #### 12.4.2.1 Assertion-based debugging
    
    Assertion-based debugging is a methodology where developers embed specific statements, or assertions, within the quantum code to define expected behaviors and outcomes. These assertions act as checkpoints, serving as benchmarks for the quantum program’s execution. During debugging, the system automatically verifies whether these assertions hold true. If an assertion fails, it signals a deviation from the expected behavior, highlighting potential errors or bugs in the code. This approach enhances the developer’s ability to systematically and proactively validate the correctness of quantum programs, allowing for early detection and resolution of issues before they become more complex or problematic.
    
    There are three essential types of quantum assertions within the realm of quantum computing. First, classical assertions are utilized to confirm the proper interaction of classical components in hybrid quantum-classical systems with quantum processes. Second, superposition assertions employ propositional logic implication to assess the presence of a uniform quantum superposition state. Last but not least, entanglement assertions serve a vital role in detecting errors in quantum circuits by ensuring the persistence of specific entangled states, a crucial task due to the fragile nature of quantum systems. Together, these assertion types collectively contribute to the verification and maintenance of desired quantum behaviors and states in quantum programs.
    
    Recent advancements in quantum computing validation, particularly the introduction of quantum assertions, represent a significant stride in the field. Quantum assertions play a pivotal role in verifying the functionality of quantum programs and addressing the intricate challenges associated with their development. Li et al. \[[30](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_030)\] state that quantum assertions act as a vital tool for debugging quantum programs and mitigating hardware errors, addressing the unique challenges presented by quantum properties like the no-cloning theorem and potentially destructive measurements. Through projection-based monitoring and statistical assertion techniques, quantum assertions offer a promising avenue not only for debugging but also for error mitigation and lightweight error correction. In essence, quantum assertions offer a compelling solution to improve the dependability of quantum computing systems, marking a substantial step forward in the quantum computing landscape.
    
    The work by Huang and Martonosi \[[28](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_028)\] outlines a comprehensive strategy for deploying and verifying quantum program assertions through statistical testing. It addresses diverse bug types and defense types critical for improving the reliability and correctness of quantum programs. It also introduces contingency table analysis as a powerful tool for validating intermediate states, especially in quantum chemistry simulations. The experiment extends across multiple case studies. In the context of quantum chemistry simulations, precise classical input parameters and qubit initial values are paramount, and precondition assertions serve to validate these inputs. Postcondition assertions focus on preserving symmetry in quantum models. Furthermore, Grover’s search algorithm \[[64](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_064)\] demonstrates how higher-level quantum development platforms, like ProjectQ \[[65](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_065)\], simplify the placement of assertions, particularly in the context of controlled operations. Shor’s algorithm \[[66](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_066)\] emphasizes the importance of product state assertions to validate the correct deallocation of ancillary qubits in quantum subroutines. These findings underscore the need for structured debugging methodologies, including assertion checks, in quantum program development.
    
    In a series of experiments conducted on a 20-qubit IBMQ QC, Liu et al. \[[31](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_031)\] explore the impact of dynamic runtime assertions in quantum computing. The study investigates various types of assertions and integrates them into different quantum algorithms like quantum Fourier transform \[[67](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_067)\], quantum phase estimation \[[68](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_068)\], Bernstein Vaziani \[[69](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_069)\], and more. The study also introduces the concept of asserting deallocated ancillary qubits to maintain program correctness. The results indicate a marked improvement in quantum algorithm precision when executed on real quantum hardware. The open-source Qiskit framework incorporates these assertion circuits for broader quantum computing community access.
    
    The work by Li et al. \[[29](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_029)\] introduces Proq, a projection-based runtime assertion scheme that uses projection operators as predicates in assert primitives. Proq claims to enhance expressive power and reduce assertion overhead compared to existing quantum assertion schemes. The study encompasses theoretical foundations and practical transformations to make projection-based assertions feasible on measurement-restricted QCs. Although Proq is promising, the work lacks empirical validation of the Proq scheme through practical demonstrations or experiments on real-world quantum programs. Also, the complexity introduced by using projection operators may pose a challenge, especially for quantum developers without a deep understanding of quantum mechanics.
    
    Metwalli and Van Meter \[[70](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_070)\] propose the Quantum Circuit Slicing tool, drawing inspiration from manual slicing and breakpoints. Quantum Circuit Slicing tool simplifies complex quantum circuits by dividing them into manageable slices for in-depth examination and debugging. A beta version of the tool was released for testing to a group of developers, who found it invaluable for simplifying circuits, enhancing their understanding, and locating bugs through the gate tracking feature. While the results are promising, the work could benefit from more detailed explanations of experiments and findings.
    
    Witharana et al. \[[71](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_071)\] present an automated approach for generating quantum assertions to validate quantum circuits. The performing of quAssert involves two stages, that is, mining assertions and inserting assertions. In the first stage, quAssert can automatically uncover special properties of a quantum circuit, such as superposition, and entangled states, using statistical methods. These uncovered properties indicate the locations for inserting assertions. Then, in stage 2, quAssert will place quantum assertions automatically and maximize coverage. The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of quAssert using a suite of quantum benchmarks including Shor’s algorithm and Grover’s algorithm. The results show that automatically inserted assertions can expose bugs efficiently, for example, it can quickly detect a quantum bug with 15 assertions.
    
    #### 12.4.2.2 Interactive debugging
    
    Interactive debugging is a real-time debugging technique that helps developers identify and rectify bugs as they run the program. The instantaneous insights of bugs provided in interactive debugging make it a rapid and time-efficient debugging strategy. However, it may not be suitable for quantum programs because of the well-known quantum measurement problem (i.e., wave function collapse).
    
    The composer from IBM quantum platform \[[63](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_063)\] provides an interactive debugging environment, enhancing the debugging process for quantum circuits. Particularly, the platform offers an “inspect” functionality within the composer, enabling a thorough inspection of the circuit gate-by-gate or layer-by-layer.
    
    In [Figure 12.1](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_fig_001), there is an example that indicates using the “inspect” feature to debug a two-qubit quantum circuit. As you can see from the figure, this feature allows users to observe the functioning of the circuit through each gate or layer by clicking the “next” button in the composer.
    
    ![Figure 12.1: A quantum circuit in the composer of IBM quantum platform, showing the “inspect” progress. Developers can inspect the quantum circuit step-by-step.](graphic/converted/b_9783111342276-012_fig_001.jpg)
    
    Figure 12.1: A quantum circuit in the composer of IBM quantum platform, showing the “inspect” progress. Developers can inspect the quantum circuit step-by-step.
    
    As the debugger, “inspect” steps through the gates or layers of the quantum circuit, users can analyze the changes in the outcome probability for every computational quantum state, as depicted in [Figure 12.2](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_fig_002). As you can see from the figure, during the process of “inspect,” developers can check the probabilities of the quantum states instantly.
    
    ![Figure 12.2: During the “inspect” process, a developer can check the probabilities of quantum states. In this example, we have two qubits, and the quantum states spread from 00, 01, 10, to 11, with similar probabilities.](graphic/converted/b_9783111342276-012_fig_002.jpg)
    
    Figure 12.2: During the “inspect” process, a developer can check the probabilities of quantum states. In this example, we have two qubits, and the quantum states spread from 00, 01, 10, to 11, with similar probabilities.
    
    Furthermore, [Figure 12.3](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_fig_003) illustrates the Q-Sphere representation, another valuable tool for visualizing the quantum states during the debugging process. The Q-Sphere provides a comprehensive view of the quantum state, allowing users to gain insights into the state’s evolution and potential issues.
    
    ![Figure 12.3: During the “inspect” process, a developer can see the Q-sphere, which offers a visualization of multiqubit states along with their phase and amplitudes.](graphic/converted/b_9783111342276-012_fig_003.jpg)
    
    Figure 12.3: During the “inspect” process, a developer can see the Q-sphere, which offers a visualization of multiqubit states along with their phase and amplitudes.
    
    #### 12.4.2.3 Debugging in local IDEs
    
    Debugging tools are available in integrated development environments (IDEs), such as PyCharm (for Python programming, JetBrains \[[72](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_072)\]). The demonstration of [Figure 12.4](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_fig_004) involves importing Qiskit, creating a simple quantum code, and intentionally inducing errors to test the debugging features. Two key characteristics of the debugging process are highlighted.
    
    ![Figure 12.4: An example of a quantum program in Qiskit with interactive debugging provided by PyCharm.](graphic/converted/b_9783111342276-012_fig_004.jpg)
    
    Figure 12.4: An example of a quantum program in Qiskit with interactive debugging provided by PyCharm.
    
    In [Figure 12.4](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_fig_004), breakpoints are utilized for interactive debugging. Breakpoints act as temporary stops in the code, instructing the debugger to pause before executing the subsequent line. This facilitates a detailed analysis of the code at the designated breakpoint, aiding in effective debugging. The red circle in the code’s left sidebar signifies the introduced breakpoint.
    
    Upon activation of the debugger, [Figure 12.5](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_fig_005) demonstrates how the debugger displays detailed information about variables up to the breakpoint. This information is presented in the lower area of the window, enabling users to evaluate variables and understand the code’s state at the breakpoint.
    
    ![Figure 12.5: A developer can evaluate variables during debugging in PyCharm.](graphic/converted/b_9783111342276-012_fig_005.jpg)
    
    Figure 12.5: A developer can evaluate variables during debugging in PyCharm.
    
    The second feature involves “code inspections,” as illustrated in [Figure 12.6](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_fig_006). This functionality provides debugging tips whenever an error is identified. When the mouse hovers over the line containing the error, a pop-up reveals information about the type of error and offers debugging recommendations. In this context, a bug related to incorrect arguments for the circuit gate is discovered, and the debugging actions are provided for resolution.
    
    ![Figure 12.6: A syntax error (i.e., unexpected argument) can be detected by code inspections in PyCharm.](graphic/converted/b_9783111342276-012_fig_006.jpg)
    
    Figure 12.6: A syntax error (i.e., unexpected argument) can be detected by code inspections in PyCharm.
    
    To summarize, debugging techniques (e.g., assertion-based debugging and interactive debugging) have been developed in QSE. However, due to the nature of quantum mechanics, both the literature and the industry require more novel proposals and tools to debug quantum programs more efficiently and effectively (just like the tools that developers are using in classical SE).
    
    ## 12.5 Challenges and opportunities
    
    Both research challenges and opportunities exist in the field of QSE. In this section, we will highlight some of the key challenges and opportunities.
    
    ### 12.5.1 QSE research challenges
    
    We present three major challenges in testing and debugging quantum software as follows:
    
    *   **First, the literature in QSE lacks automation for fixing quantum software bugs.** Bug pattern detection is a common static approach to analyze bugs. It helps developers understand common bug categories and characteristics. Later on, those characteristics can be used to develop tools that can scan source code to find bugs. In classical SE, previous studies show that bug pattern analysis and detection can help in developing automated techniques to detect bugs from existing source code more effectively and efficiently \[[73](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_073)\]. However, the research on bug patterns in QSE is still immature. Previous studies of bug patterns (such as Bugs4Q by Zhao et al. \[[35](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_035)\]) and bug-fix patterns (e.g., Q-Diff \[[37](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_037)\]) need to be extended to the next step. For example, the characteristics of bugs can be used as inputs for classification or clustering models (e.g., support vector machine and K-means) to detect bug patterns.
        
    *   Our initial step in bug-fix pattern detection \[[37](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_037)\] can be used to predict fix patterns for existing quantum software bugs. Another challenge for QSE is the lack of data. This leads to the fact that the datasets for training machine learning models in QSE are small. We can use simple machine learning models (decision trees and support vector machine), cross-validation, and semi-supervised learning approaches for limited data.
        
    *   **Second, it is challenging to test quantum software because of its indeterminacy.** As mentioned in Quantum software testing section, indeterminacy is the nature of quantum computing. However, indeterminacy brings significant challenges to testing and debugging quantum programs. For example, quantum programs produce probabilistic results because of randomness. Such cases can cause flaky tests, which means that the same test case can fail in some cases and pass in other cases. In reality, enterprises suffer from flaky tests, for example, 4.5% of test cases fail because of flaky tests in Google \[[12](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_012)\].
        
    *   **Last but not least, interactive debugging in quantum computing poses unique challenges.** Quantum programs operate in superposition, making observation complex due to state collapse risks. Entanglement adds complexity as debugging requires managing correlated qubits. Limited classical information, probabilistic outcomes, and complex gate sequences further complicate debugging. Developing effective debugging tools tailored to quantum systems is challenging. Scalability concerns arise with larger quantum systems, and real-time interaction introduces issues like latency and responsiveness. Specialized debugging tools and methodologies are essential for ensuring the reliability and correctness of quantum software.
        
    
    ### 12.5.2 Future research directions in QSE
    
    We highlight three research opportunities/directions with challenges for researchers:
    
    *   **First, leverage classical SE best practices, especially those techniques that have been proven to be effective in dynamic programming and machine learning because of their random nature.** Classical SE has developed for multiple decades. Methods and tools are widely studied in all areas of SE such as requirements engineering, system modeling, compliance, secure engineering, verification, validation, maintenance, and evolution. Since QSE is still immature, one can leverage classical SE best practices on QSE. More specifically, the indeterminacy of quantum computing is similar to dynamic or stochastic programming. Thus, testing techniques (e.g., search-based software testing \[[21](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_021)\]) that are proven to be effective in these domains can be potentially applied to QSE.
        
    *   **Second, incorporate bug-fix pattern detection into debugging to predict fix patterns for existing bugs.** Due to the short history of QSE and quantum software development, existing research on QSE is mainly on bug pattern analysis \[[11](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_011)\]. However, in classical SE, bug pattern analysis is usually the initial step for automated fixing. A typical research agenda for automated fixing is (1) bug pattern analysis, (2) bug pattern classification, (3) bug-fix pattern analysis, and (4) fix prediction for existing bugs. Our previous study on bug-fix pattern recognition is the first research on Step 3. The next step is to utilize classical clustering or classification algorithms (such as SVM \[[74](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_074)\] and K-means \[[75](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_075)\]) and artificial neural networks (such as LSTM \[[76](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_076)\] and GRU \[[77](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_077)\]) to predict a fix pattern for an existing bug in quantum software.
        
    *   **Last but not list, combine artificial intelligence (AI) and quantum computing.** In the dynamic landscape of computer science, quantum computing, and AI stand as groundbreaking milestones. Quantum computing, with its unparalleled computational capabilities, is redefining the boundaries of what is computationally feasible. Concurrently, the advent of neurosymbolic AI, integration of symbolic and neural techniques,[¹](#fn1)
        
        ## 12.6 Conclusions
        
        QSE is a “child” community in SE, and it has a relatively short history compared to the history of its “parent.” However, because of the potential of quantum computing and recent breakthroughs in quantum hardware, the research and development of quantum software are rapidly evolving. More and more researchers and practitioners joined the community, and they started bringing SE best practices into the world of quantum computing. On October 30, 2023, President Biden directed the most sweeping actions to protect Americans from the potential risks of AI systems. These actions include but are not limited to developing standards, tools, and tests to help ensure that AI systems are safe, secure, and trustworthy \[[78](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_078)\]. As the power of QCs is combined with AI models (e.g., quantum neural networks proposed by Altaisky \[[79](#9783111342092_022341.xhtml#b_9783111342276-012_ref_079)\]), it is more urgent than ever to harness quantum AI software through standards and testing against classical and quantum threats.
        
        In this chapter, we conduct a survey of testing and debugging methods and tools for quantum software. We analyze bug patterns, bug pattern detections, testing tactics, and debugging tactics. We also highlight some common and popular techniques in quantum software testing and debugging. We discuss the challenges and opportunities for the research field of QC software. The ultimate goal is to automate the process of testing and debugging quantum software to speed up development and improve its quality. We believe that this work would be of interest to practitioners creating quantum programs as well as researchers developing the next generations of tooling for QC.
