    ## Notes
    
    # 13 Quantum-enhanced neural networks: bridging the quantum algorithm and machine learning
    
    Manisa Manoswini Debasish Swapnesh Kumar Nayak Tejaswini Das Tripti Swarnkar 
    
    #### Abstract
    
    Using quantum concepts like superposition and entanglement, quantum-enhanced neural networks (QENN) combine machine learning (ML) and quantum computing to improve neural network performance. They are expected to have a revolutionary influence on optimization, pattern recognition, and complex data analysis. In this work, authors discuss the principles of quantum computing, including superposition, entanglement, and quantum gates, before introducing many quantum ML (QML) techniques, including Grover’s search, Shor’s factoring, and quantum annealing. Following that, it discusses the fundamentals of neural networks for problems like image recognition and natural language processing while addressing issues with big data and high-dimensional features. This work focuses on using quantum processors to speed up traditional ML tasks by combining quantum algorithms with neural networks to generate hybrid models. It investigates quantum kernel techniques, quantum variational algorithms, and QENN, demonstrating their potential to improve conventional neural network frameworks. In addition to this authors also include real-world applications in fields including drug development, materials research, finance, and optimization to show the revolutionary effects of quantum-enhanced machine learning (QEML) on current problems. In conclusion, this chapter provides a thorough review of QML, highlighting the synergy between quantum algorithms and neural networks. It offers unique insights into the field’s current state, practical applications, and hopes for defining the future of artificial intelligence through quantum computational paradigms.
    
    Keywords:  Quantum computing,  quantum-enhanced neural networks,  machine learning,  convolutional neural network,  high-dimension features,  
    
    ## 13.1 Introduction
    
    The development of quantum machine learning (QML) is evidence of human curiosity and the never-ending quest for new ideas. Quantum physics altered our knowledge of the fundamental components of the cosmos around the turn of the twentieth century. By frequently pushing the limits of classical physics, pioneers like Max Planck, Albert Einstein, and Niels Bohr established the foundation for what would eventually become quantum computing \[[1](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_ref_001), [2](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_ref_002), [3](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_ref_003)\].
    
    A unique abstraction has developed in the dynamic world of technology and artificial intelligence, and it has the potential to revolutionize how authors will approach optimization, pattern recognition, and complex data analysis. Quantum-enhanced neural network (QENN) is a revolutionary paradigm that combines the strength of quantum computing with the intelligence of neural networks to open up a range of possibilities that were previously only possible in science fiction. This abstraction has the potential to transform machine learning (ML) and push the boundaries of what authors thought was possible by utilizing the special principles of quantum physics \[[4](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_ref_004)\].
    
    The development of QML is evidence of human curiosity and the never-ending quest for new ideas. Quantum physics altered our knowledge of the fundamental components of the cosmos around the turn of the twentieth century. By frequently pushing the limits of classical physics, pioneers like Max Planck, Albert Einstein, and Niels Bohr established the foundation for what would eventually become quantum computing.
    
    The concept of quantum computing began to take shape in the second phase of the twentieth centennial. The groundbreaking concept of Richard Feynman, which involved simulating quantum phenomena using quantum systems, captured the attention of scientists and computer engineers. Quantum algorithms, the brilliant creations of luminaries like David Deutsch, Lov Grover, and Peter Shor, began to take shape in the following decades. These algorithms provide unheard-of speeds for resolving issues that were intractable by conventional computers \[[5](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_ref_005)\].
    
    Successful advancement has been made in the domain of computational intelligence, notably in the domain of ML, in recent years \[[6](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_ref_006), [7](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_ref_007)\]. The potential of learning machines is amply illustrated by a recent accomplishment that was previously believed to be decades away – a computer beating a skilled human player at Go \[[8](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_ref_008)\]. The foundations of a brand-new field termed QML are being laid in parallel with this. The field of artificial intelligence could be completely transformed by it, just as quantum information processing has done for its conventional counterpart \[[9](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_ref_009)\]. The emergence of neural networks and ML heralded a new age in artificial intelligence, coinciding with advances in quantum computing. It became clearer that neural networks were capable of tackling challenging tasks, for example, NLP and picture identification. Four key players are involved with the introduction of NISQ (noisy intermediate-scale quantum) technology as shown in [Figure 13.1](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_fig_001).
    
    ![Figure 13.1: Study area of NISQ technology.](graphic/converted/b_9783111342276-013_fig_001.jpg)
    
    Figure 13.1: Study area of NISQ technology.
    
    The real work of creating qubits and the associated hardware is handled by engineers and physicists, while mathematicians, computer scientists, and theorists in general are occupied with expanding the boundaries of quantum information and quantum algorithms.
    
    Quantum computing might deliver benefits in the sectors of ML and neural networks. With a slew of game-changing advantages, quantum computing holds immense potential in the domains of ML and neural networks. Fundamentally, the capacity for achieving exponential speedup in particular computations stands out as a game-changer, with quantum algorithms like Grover’s search and Shor’s factoring poised to outperform their classical counterparts and potentially speeding up ML tasks like data search and factorization. The parallelism provided by superposition, in which quantum bits can concurrently exist in multiple states, further boosts its prowess. This remarkable ability of quantum computers to explore numerous problem solutions concurrently is beneficial for optimization and pattern recognition tasks in ML. It is also the phenomenon targeted by specialized QML algorithms.
    
    The Quantum Approximate Optimization Algorithm (QAQA) is one example of a quantum variational algorithm that brings improved efficiency to solving optimization issues and promises performance improvements across various ML tasks. The consequences of quantum computing for encryption, driven by decomposition and search capabilities, demonstrate the need for cryptography after quantum computing for ML protection of systems at identical times. Quantum computers also focus on energy economy and offer resource frugality for certain activities, which is advantageous for ML applications on devices with limited resources. In conclusion, the incorporation of quantum-enhanced techniques into ML and neural networks offers some degree of future-proofing as quantum computing develops, positioning itself strategically to adapt to developing quantum technologies, and leaving an enduring imprint on the future of artificial intelligence.
    
    ### 13.1.1 Motive and background
    
    This chapter sets out on a journey through the complex world of quantum computing, concentrating on the fundamental ideas behind this groundbreaking discipline. Its core ideas – superposition, entanglement, and quantum gates – serve as the cornerstone for the subsequent quantum algorithms. These techniques, such as Grover’s search, Shor’s factoring, and quantum annealing, have the potential to completely alter the ML environment by providing unheard-of computational benefits.
    
    Additionally, the core of this chapter is grounded in tackling the enormous difficulties and complexity faced by conventional neural networks in the fluid environment of modern artificial intelligence. Quantum computing appears as a powerful ally at a time characterized by an enormous influx of data and the deep complexity of high-dimensional feature spaces. Its ability to simultaneously analyze enormous amounts of data opens the door to speeding up typical ML tasks and ushers in a new era in which quantum and classical computing combine to achieve previously unimaginable results \[[10](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_ref_010)\].
    
    Our main concern in this part is developing hybrid models that seamlessly combine the fields of quantum computing and neural networks. These novel models, such as quantum kernel methods, quantum variational algorithms, and quantum-enhanced neural networks, are closely examined. They present the intriguing possibility of utilizing the special advantages of quantum computing to not only accelerate but also dramatically improve conventional neural network frameworks.
    
    This chapter leaves the theoretical sphere and enters the domain of application by investigating concrete real-world uses of QEML. These applications cover important areas like medication discovery, materials science, financial modeling, and problems with optimization. Here, researchers see the practical and revolutionary potential of QEML in solving current real-world issues and grasping fresh chances for innovation.
    
    Finally, the purpose of this chapter is to go into the frontier of QML and demonstrate the complicated relationship between quantum algorithms and neural networks. It provides clear insights into the current status of the science, relevant applications, and a vision for molding artificial intelligence via the prism of quantum computing paradigms. This journey takes place against a backdrop of infinite possibilities, where the interwoven worlds of quantum theory and ML define the cutting-edge of modern science, promising revolutionary advances in the area.
    
    ## 13.2 Quantum principles
    
    Quantum computing examines information processing utilizing quantum mechanical systems. Quantum computers use qubits, which can simultaneously represent the combination of 0 and 1, enabling exponentially more computing possibilities than traditional computers that handle individual bits. Qubits are capable of carrying out complicated calculations that are beyond the scope of conventional computers because of quantum features like superposition and entanglement. Even the most sophisticated supercomputers may not be able to tackle the computational issues that this prospective platform could tackle. The foundations of quantum computing, alternative qubit representations, and how quantum features are used for computation are all covered in this section.
    
    ### 13.2.1 Superposition
    
    A basic notion in quantum computing is superposition, which allows quantum particles, or qubits, to be present at the same time in different states. Keeping things simple, the idea of a qubit will be taught using the illustration of a photon in this section. This example shows that one bit of data is encoded by the polarization of a photon, which is related to its geometric direction. Specifically, a photon that is horizontally polarized represents a traditional bit 0, while a photon that is vertically polarized represents a traditional bit 1 \[[11](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_ref_011)\].
    
    In [Figure 13.1](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_fig_001), photons are generated and then flow through a section known as Filter-V, which only allows photons that are vertically polarized to pass through. To simplify the scenario, authors suppose that a single photon enters the setup at a time. The next part, the polarization beam splitter (PBS), works like this: For vertically polarized photons, the detector marked DV is used for transmission, while for horizontally polarized photons, the detector named DH is used for redirection and measurement. Consequently, all incoming photons in this setup ([Figure 13.2](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_fig_002)) will exhibit their vertical polarization when measured at DV.
    
    ![Figure 13.2: Picture found at DV.](graphic/converted/b_9783111342276-013_fig_002.png)
    
    Figure 13.2: Picture found at DV.
    
    On the other hand, according to [Figure 13.3](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_fig_003), if “Filter-45” is placed in between Filter-V and PBS, each detector has a 12% probability of detecting a horizontally or vertically divided photon.
    
    ![Figure 13.3: One half of the image is in DV, and the other half is at DH.](graphic/converted/b_9783111342276-013_fig_003.png)
    
    Figure 13.3: One half of the image is in DV, and the other half is at DH.
    
    The probability amplitudes for each should be 1/2, since the chance of finding either vertically or horizontally polarized photons at the PBS is 1/2. As a result, the photon’s condition just before PBS can be shown as
    
    (13.1)$|\\psi› = 1/\\sqrt{}2\\ |H› + 1\\ /\\sqrt{}2\\ |V›$ 
    
    Since the bit “1” in expression (13.1) is represented by |V*)* and the bit “0” by |H*)*, respectively, the expression is expressed as
    
    (13.2)$|\\psi› = 1/\\sqrt{}2\\ |0›) + 1\\ /\\sqrt{}2\\ |1›$ 
    
    In this state of preparation, a photon’s digital information is either “0” or “1.” It is possible to interpret eq. (13.2) as indicating that the states “0” and “1” are both present simultaneously. This unique circumstance is known as a superposition condition.
    
    In the superposition state (5), two qubits are shown as follows:
    
    (13.3)$\\begin{matrix} {|\\psi 1›\\psi 2›} & {= \\{ 1\\sqrt{}2\\ |0› + 1\\sqrt{}2\\ |1›\\}\\ \\{ 1\\sqrt{}2\\ |0› + 1\\sqrt{}2\\ |1›\\}} \\\\ & {= 1\\ 2\\ \\{\\left\\lbrack {1,1} \\right\\rbrack\\ T \\otimes \\left\\lbrack {1,1} \\right\\rbrack\\ T\\} = 1\\ 2\\ \\left\\lbrack {1,1,1,1} \\right\\rbrack\\ T} \\\\ & {= 1\\ 2\\ \\left\\{ {\\left\\lbrack {1,0,0,0} \\right\\rbrack\\ T + \\left\\lbrack {0,1,0,0} \\right\\rbrack\\ T + \\left\\lbrack {0,0,1,0} \\right\\rbrack\\ T + \\left\\lbrack {0,0,0,1} \\right\\rbrack\\ T} \\right\\}} \\\\ & {= 1\\ 2\\ |0›|0› + 1\\ 2\\ |0›|1› + 1\\ 2\\ |1›|0› + 1\\ 2\\ |1›|1›} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ 
    
    (00, 01, 10, and 11) are the four classical binaries inherited from the two-qubit state occurring concurrently. The details of quantum circuits with *n* qubits are shown in [Figure 13.4](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_fig_004).
    
    ![Figure 13.4: The quantum circuit with n qubits.](graphic/converted/b_9783111342276-013_fig_004.jpg)
    
    Figure 13.4: The quantum circuit with *n* qubits.
    
    ## 13.3 Entanglement: a fascinating phenomenon of quantum physics
    
    One of the most useful tools in quantum computing is entanglement. Qubits can share information and carry out intricate operations in ways that regular bits cannot when they are entangled. This characteristic is crucial for developing quantum algorithms, which can solve some problems significantly more quickly than conventional algorithms \[[12](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_ref_012)\]. In the 1970s, computer reversibility emerged as a prominent area of discussion. One key concept in computing is reversible computing, which lets you go back from an output state to the input state. This idea consists of two separate components:
    
    ## 13.4 Physical reversibility
    
    Physical reversibility indicates the fact that once an input is removed, the data is irreversibly lost. Logical reversibility, on the other hand, emphasizes the capacity to reform the data from the outcome. The NAND gate is unalterable because it is impossible to recreate the inputs from the outcome data. The NAND gate has a single output and two inputs. The NOT gate, on the other hand, is reversible \[[13](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_ref_013)\].
    
    Because its input and output are mapped one-to-one, reversible logic guarantees that no data is lost and no energy is wasted. Quantum gates are a significant advantage of quantum computing due to their reversibility. With quantum computers, it is possible to avoid the irreversibility that accompanies classical processing and avoid information loss. Quantum gates are the building components of quantum circuits in the computation model known as quantum circuits \[[14](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_ref_014)\]. Qubits are subjected to typical gates and operations. The single-qubit quantum gates that include H, X, Y, Z, T, and I, as well as the Phase gate, are basic and significant. Swap, CZ, and CNOT gates are two-qubit quantum gates; Toffoli and Fredkin gates are three-qubit quantum gates. Single-qubit and two-qubit quantum gates are utilized by traditional logic gates like NAND, AND, NOR, XOR, and OR. A gate with *n* qubits is represented as a 2*n* × 2*n* matrix. In contrast, 4 × 4 matrices represent two-qubit gates, and single-qubit gates are represented as 2 × 2 matrices.
    
    |0› → *A*|0› + *B*|1› |1› → *C*|0› + *D*|1› 
    
    The aforementioned operation can be› expressed in a matrix as follows:
    
    $\\begin{matrix} {A\\ C} \\\\ {B\\ D} \\\\ \\ \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ 
    
    ### 13.4.1 Interaction and measure of qubits
    
    The interaction with qubits is in the context of different quantum computer physical realizations like neutral atoms and spin qubits. It details the manipulation and measurement of qubits in these systems as follows:
    
    *   **Neutral atom realization****:** This method represents qubits by using the electron quantum states of neutral atoms. An electron can be in one of two states: excited (mapped to |1) or ground (mapped to |0). An electron can move between these states by exposing the atom to a specified energy level for a set amount of time. The electron may also be put in a superposition state, where it is simultaneously in |0 and |1 by altering the illumination time.
        
    *   **Realization of spin qubits****:** Spin qubits are yet another physical embodiment of qubits. A powerful magnetic field is used to initialize them in a particular state, usually all spin-up (representing the 0 state), at the start. Electromagnetic pulses are used to individually control each of these qubits. The applied electromagnetic pulse’s amplitude and length determine the qubit’s state.
        
    
    Due to the fundamental laws of quantum mechanics, continuous measurement at every place is not possible in quantum computing. Because there is inherent uncertainty in every measurement, it is impossible to take continuous measurements. It is essential to have a physical apparatus that can precisely measure all of the qubit states involved in the computation in order to obtain the outcome of quantum computations.
    
    Preparing quantum systems for computations, ensuring they remain in a coherent state, and carrying out measurements that produce useful data from the quantum states are the key hurdles in quantum computing. This is crucial for understanding and applying the quantum data produced by the computation. To fully utilize the capabilities of quantum computing technology, these obstacles must be overcome.
    
    ## 13.5 Techniques for QML
    
    ML is based on teaching computers to learn via data processing and analysis processes. This field combines artificial intelligence with computational statistics at the intersection of computer science and statistics. A subset of classical ML called deep learning (both supervised and unsupervised) has proven to be very helpful for tasks like speech recognition, image classification, pattern detection, and managing large datasets. It has consequently attracted a lot of interest and funding from numerous companies.
    
    However, there is an increasing demand for fresh strategies to automatically manage, organize, and categorize the vast amounts of data authors encounter every day. Traditional ML is adaptive and agile and excels at seeing patterns, but it may not be able to address some complicated problems effectively.
    
    Businesses that handle large database administration are aware of these drawbacks and are eager to explore other methods. One approach is to use QML. In order to potentially facilitate more efficient problem-solving, QML aims to integrate ML algorithms within quantum systems. It achieves this by utilizing entanglement and superposition, two examples of quantum properties.
    
    Quantum computers exploit superposition states to allow for the simultaneous execution of several tasks. This provides a definite speed and data processing advantage over standard ML methods. QML uses quantum algorithms to perform classical algorithm-intensive tasks on a quantum computer. This enables both unsupervised and supervised algorithms to be used to classify, categorize, and analyze data. Models such as support vector machines or quantum neural networks are frequently used to implement these strategies. As an outcome, QML has the capability to totally revolutionize how authors handle and evaluate data, opening up new opportunities for application development \[[16](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_ref_016)\]. A brief explanation of each of the listed quantum machines learning techniques may be found in the following section.
    
    ### 13.5.1 Grover’s search: quantum space search revolutionized
    
    Grover’s algorithm is an unstructured search quantum algorithm that uses only *O*(*N*) function evaluations, where *N* is the size of the function’s domain, to identify the unique data to a black box operation that yields a given output value. Lov Grover invented it in 1996 \[[17](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_ref_017)\]. Because one must often explore half the domain to discover the proper input, Umesh Vazirani, Gilles Brassard, Ethan Bernstein, and Charles H. Bennett demonstrated that the function (*N*) must be evaluated there to be a quantum solution to the issue.
    
    Grover’s technique, according to Ethan Bernstein, Gilles Brassard, Charles H. Bennett and Umesh Vazirani, is asymptotically optimum since evaluating the function (*N*) times is required for any quantum solution of the issue \[[18](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_ref_018)\]. Grover’s approach to unstructured search only provides a quadratic speedup over solution; this implies that Grover’s algorithm will not solve NP-complete problems in polynomial time, because the square root of an exponential function is an exponential function rather than a polynomial function \[[9](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_ref_009)\]. Grover’s approach only provides a quadratic speedup over other quantum algorithms, which may provide an exponential speedup over their conventional counterparts. Grover’s approach may accelerate a wide range of tasks, and even quadratic acceleration becomes noticeable when *N* is big.
    
    Grover’s approach can brute-force a 256-bit key in 2,128 iterations and a 128-bit key in around 264 iterations. However, Grover’s technique may not represent a considerably bigger danger to encryption than commonly utilized classical methods \[[19](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_ref_019)\].
    
    ### 13.5.2 Application and drawbacks
    
    Many calculations can be accelerated using Grover’s approach, and modifications like amplitude amplification \[[20](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_ref_020), [21](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_ref_021), [22](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_ref_022)\], specifically an exhaustive search subroutine, are usually included in algorithms for NP-complete problems, and Grover’s method helps to speed this up \[[15](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_ref_015)\]. Among them is the greatest 3SAT approach available right now. In \[[23](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_ref_023)\], the input does not need to be provided in the form of an oracle for these techniques because Grover’s method is used with an explicit function, like verifying if a collection of bits fulfills a 3SAT instance.
    
    ### 13.5.3 Cryptography
    
    Grover’s approach is used to address the function inversion problem. For instance, function like *y* =* f*(*x*), which can be evaluated on a quantum computer, can use Grover’s approach to compute *x* given *y*. Grover’s approach significantly accelerates a variety of encryption techniques, including collision and preimage attacks \[[24](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_ref_024)\]. Because the parallel rho algorithm, for example, may discover a collision in SHA2 quicker than Grover’s technique, this may not be the most effective option \[[25](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_ref_025)\].
    
    *   **Drawbacks:** The technique was initially described as a database search algorithm in Grover’s article, and this usage persists to this day. In this analogy, the database is a table that is indexed by the pertinent input and contains every output produced by the function. However, there is no explicit display of this database. Rather, an oracle is contacted to evaluate a piece of information based on its index. The time it takes to go through an entire storage item by item and convert it to this specification may outrun Grover’s search. Grover’s technique might be viewed of as satisfying a constraint or solving an equation to account for such consequences. In these cases, the oracle is not used in the search process; rather, it is used to validate the constraint.
        
    
    Traditional search algorithms frequently rely on such optimizations to avoid exhaustive searches, but this separation typically prevents algorithmic optimizations \[[26](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_ref_026)\]. Grover’s oracle is simple to construct and can solve a wide range of constraint fulfillment and optimization issues \[[27](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_ref_027)\].
    
    The fundamental impediment to the adoption of Grover’s technique is that the resulting quadratic speedup is insufficient to compensate for the considerable cost of short-term quantum computing \[[28](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_ref_028)\]. These data speedups may be attainable in subsequent generations of fault-tolerant quantum machines with improved hardware performance.
    
    ## 13.6 Oracle
    
    Consider an unorganized area of search with *N* items in which authors want to find a certain element. Rather than looking for a specific element, authors assign each of these items an index, which is just an integer between 0 and *N* 1. Researchers can retain generality while assuming *N* = 2*n* (*n* € *Z*) and exactly M answers (1 *M N*) to this search issue. Furthermore, authors create a selection function *f* that takes as input a state expressing an integer between 0 and *N* 1 (*xi*). When the status is an answer to the research issue, value 1 is assigned; alternatively, value 0 is assigned \[[29](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_ref_029)\].
    
    Given a quantum oracle-black box (a unitary operator *O*), authors may calculate its effect on the computing base using the following formula:
    
    (13.4)|*x*)|*q*) → |*x*)|*q*⁰⊕ *f*(*x*)) 
    
    The index register in the above equation is |*x)*.
    
    ### 13.6.1 Algorithm
    
    To begin, construct a superposition of *N* quantum states via a Hadamard transformation:
    
    $(H^{\\otimes n}\\ {where}\\ H =^{\\sqrt{\\,}}1/2\\, 1\\, 1\\ {on}\\ |0i^{\\otimes n}.\\, 1 - 1$ 1 − 1 (13.5)$|\\psi) = 1/\\sqrt{}N\_{\\;\\;\\; x\\! = \\! 0}^{\\Sigma\_{N\\! - \\! 1}}|x)$ 
    
    When the first superposition is finished, the procedure continues by using a quantum subroutine called the Grover operator denoted by *G*, repeatedly. The following actions are part of the Grover iteration:
    
    The first step is the Grover iteration.
    
    1.  Get going
        
    2.  Make advantage of Oracle
        
    3.  Perform an inversion about the mean (*m*); that is, use *O[m] *\= 2|*ψ)* ‹*ψ)*, *I* = identity.
        
    4.  Execute transformation of *H*
        
    
    This is the first algorithm, Grover’s search.
    
    1.  Get the system going
        
    2.  Set it up first using eq. (13.3) providing its state
        
    3.  Carry out (O *√*(*N*/*M*)) times using the Grover subroutine
        
    4.  Take the subsequent state where the expected state with a probability bigger than one
        
    
    The iterative approach of Grover’s algorithm involves two critical processes: rotating the phase of the indicated state and rotating the phase around the average. These techniques can be represented by two dimensions: one for the solution space and one for the remaining search space. Grover’s approach improves the likelihood of selecting the correct answer while decreasing the likelihood of selecting the incorrect one, as seen by this geometric image \[[30](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_ref_030)\].
    
    ## 13.7 Cryptographic implications of Shor’s factoring
    
    Only prime numbers (or one) can be divided by themselves. The number system is built upon them. Because of this, almost every student with even a basic understanding of mathematics can memorize the answers to the question, “What are the prime factors, or multipliers, for the number 15?” The answers are 3 and 5. When dealing with larger numbers, like 91, a pen and paper may be required.
    
    Furthermore, experts have demonstrated high-speed classical computers working in parallel. It may take up to 2 years to factor in a bigger number, such as 232. The bulk of encryption solutions for bank cards, organizational secrets, and other sensitive data are predicated on the difficulty of factoring these numbers \[[31](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_ref_031), [32](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_ref_032)\].
    
    The method Shor uses to identify periods is based on the quantum Fourier transform (QFT). A function *f*(*x*) of the function is used by the QFT to calculate the period \[[33](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_ref_033)\].
    
    This is Shor’s algorithm:
    
    1.  After selecting a positive integer *m n* at random, with the Euclidean algorithm, the *gcd*(*m*, *n*) may be calculated in polynomial time. The prime if gcd(*m*, *n*) = after locating the factor of *n*, the issue has been resolved. Go to step 2 instead, though, if gcd(*m*, *n*) = 1.
        
    2.  Next, to find the unknown, a quantum computer is used. *x* mod *n*, *x*2 mod *n*, *x*3 mod *n*, *x*4 mod *n* are the sequences of period *P*.
        
    3.  Step 1 is repeated if it turns out that *P* is an odd number. But if *P* is an even integer, authors go to the fourth step.
        
    4.  Since *P* is an even period, (*m* ^(*P*/2) – 1) (*m* ^(*P*/2) – 1) = *m^P* – 1\= 0 mod *n*
        
    5.  Checking if *m* ^(*P*/2) + 1 = 0 mod *n* is the next step; if it is, step 1 is repeated. However, proceed to step 5 if *m* ^(*P*/2) + 1 = 0.
        
    6.  Lastly, authors obtain *d* = *gcd*(*m P*/2 – 1), *n* using the Euclidean method.
        
    
    Since *m*^(*P*/2) + 1*ƒ *\= 0 mod *n* was shown in step 4, authors can also demonstrate that *d* is a significant prime factor of *n* \[[6](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_ref_006), [32](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_ref_032)\].
    
    This is an illustration of how Shor’s algorithm works:
    
    Using Shor’s technique, *n* = 91(=7 ^✶ 13) can be factorized as follows:
    
    1.  Since *gcd*(91,3) = 1, a positive integer *m* = 3 is selected at random.
        
    2.  The period *P* is obtained via *f*(*a* = 3*a* mod 91).
        
    3.  Shor’s algorithm is used on a quantum computer to determine the period *P*, or *P* = 6. Since period *P* is even, authors move on to step 4.
        
    4.  Since the equation does not equal 0 mod 91, authors move on to step 5.
        
    5.  mod 91 3*P*/2 + 1 = 33 + 1 = 28.
        
    
    The following examples can be used to demonstrate the aforementioned algorithm: *d* is equal to *gcd*(3*P*/2 − 1, 91) = *gcd*(331, 91) = *gcd*(26,91) = *gcd*(26,91) = 1.
    
    Using a quantum computer and extensive computation, the significant prime factor of *d* = 13 of *n* = 91 was found \[[34](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_ref_034), [32](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_ref_032)\].
    
    ### 13.7.1 Quantum cryptography application areas
    
    Despite their capacity to tackle complicated tasks, the commercial availability of quantum computers is still a work in progress in the current scene. To fully realize the potential of quantum computing, hybrid platforms that combine quantum and classical computing, functioning safely in a hybrid, may be required \[[35](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_ref_035), [36](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_ref_036)\].
    
    Quantum cryptography holds potential in a variety of applications:
    
    *   **Computational chemistry****:** Quantum computers can model quantum mechanical processes, assisting in the solution of challenging material science problems such as improving catalysts, developing room-temperature superconductors, improving carbon sequestration, and constructing better-performing batteries. Fault simulation in circuits, software, and systems in quantum computing can be useful in big software and ASIC chip development, where correctness and error control are crucial. Quantum simulations can improve coverage and reduce error detection time.
        
    *   **Quantum Internet****:** A quantum Internet enables safe quantum communications. Intercontinental quantum communication experiments have set the road for future quantum Internet possibilities, with secure communication links traversing astounding distances.
        
    
    These concepts show the potential and evolving uses of quantum cryptography in various types, ranging from material research to secure communications.
    
    ## 13.8 Real-world applications
    
    Quantum computing is used in many computerized healthcare applications as part of the highly linked Internet of things (IoT) digital healthcare paradigm \[[7](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_ref_007), [33](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_ref_033)\]. Medical sensors and other networked medical equipment that can connect to the Internet or the cloud are examples of these applications \[[4](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_ref_004)\]. Quantum computers have the potential to make significant strides in the field of healthcare IoT, in addition to the enormous expansion in the processing capacity that is already benefiting this industry. The pharmaceutical industry’s shift from bits to qubits might improve medical research \[[8](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_ref_008)\]. Some of the topics covered by this research include protein folding, analyzing the strength of binding connections between a particular biomolecule, such as a cellular DNA, and its binding partner, like a drug or an inhibitor \[[11](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_ref_011), [15](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_ref_015)\], accelerating the clinical trial process, and understanding how molecules, such as drugs and enzymes, fit together. As an example, the following section gives a quick summary of a few potential uses. Customized treatment is now possible, thanks to the rapid speed at which quantum computers can now sequence DNA. Through meticulous modeling, it may make it easier to develop new medications and cures. It can also tackle difficult optimization challenges such as devising the best radiation plan to eliminate malignant cells while preserving healthy tissues in the surrounding area. Quantum computing will allow for the analysis of molecular interactions at the most fundamental step, leading to the development of new drugs and advances in medical research.
    
    ### 13.8.1 Healthcare applications of quantum computing
    
    According to recent studies, quantum computing has a distinct edge on traditional computing systems. In some usage scenarios, quantum computing can dramatically shorten calculation time frames ranging from years to minutes, speeding up illness diagnosis and treatment \[[36](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_ref_036), [37](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_ref_037)\]. It inspires new designs, tactics, and creative ways to realize a greater degree of talents for certain activities. In light of this, quantum computing has enormous potential to be used in a range of applications for the healthcare industry as a whole and for suppliers of healthcare services in particular, especially in the fields of expedited diagnosis, tailored therapy, and pricing optimization \[[38](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_ref_038)\].
    
    #### 13.8.1.1 Diagnosis support
    
    Early disease detection has the potential to significantly improve prognosis, treatment results, and healthcare expenditures. For instance, research has demonstrated that early detection of diseases like colon cancer can considerably lower treatment costs and increase survival rates. The existing diagnostic and therapeutic approaches, however, are frequently privacy, cumbersome, and prone to inaccurate diagnosis.
    
    Quantum-assisted diagnosis emerges as a promising solution, particularly in analyzing medical images and enhancing image-aided diagnosis through techniques like edge detection. Moreover, QML methods like quantum vector machines offer the ability to handle the complexities of cell classification based on biochemical and physical attributes, significantly expanding the feature space.
    
    This is vital for key diagnostics, such as discriminating between malignant and healthy cells. Quantum computing has the potential to simplify the diagnostic process by enabling continuous health monitoring, meta-analysis for cell-level diagnosis, and minimizing repetitive processes. Finally, it promises cost reductions and data-driven diagnoses that will benefit both healthcare professionals and patients \[[38](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_ref_038), [39](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_ref_039), [40](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_ref_040)\].
    
    #### 13.8.1.2 Radiotherapy
    
    Radiation therapy is an essential component of cancer care that uses radiation beams to locate and destroy malignant cells while also stopping their growth. To assure the beam’s accuracy on cancerous tissues while sparing healthy surrounding cells, precision is essential in this process \[[41](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_ref_041)\]. Modern computers control radiography, which presents a challenging optimization problem to carry out precise radiation therapy. Through simultaneous simulations and accelerated planning made possible by quantum computing, the possibilities for more effective and efficient cancer treatments are greatly increased. With improved precision and more effective treatment plans, quantum notions in simulations can open up an immense number of opportunities and perhaps revolutionize the area of radiation \[[42](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_ref_042), [43](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_ref_043), [44](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_ref_044)\].
    
    #### 13.8.1.3 Drug research and development
    
    In medical research, quantum computing is crucial because it makes it possible to model molecular interactions at the atomic level for uses in diagnostics, therapy, drug development, and analytics. Recent developments enable quantum computers to considerably outperform classical computers by encoding and simulating thousands of proteins and their interactions with medicines. With the use of techniques like bio-barcode assays, clinicians can now quickly evaluate enormous datasets, find trends, and identify disease-specific bio-markers in the blood \[[40](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_ref_040), [45](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_ref_045)\].
    
    For the pharmaceutical industry to find new drugs, accurate modeling of energy in chemical processes is essential. Due to the computationally intensive nature of calculations involving quantum phenomena, classical computers must make approximations. In contrast, quantum computers excel at accurately simulating the features of molecules, enabling accurate computations of quantum chemistry, accurate representations of wave functions, and higher estimates of molecular reaction rates. Researchers are working on quantum algorithms for chemistry, demonstrating how quantum computers can outperform classical counterparts while mimicking complicated systems and even circuitry that exhibits noise. This is a significant advance in molecular understanding and quantum-assisted research \[[41](#9783111342092_025113.xhtml#b_9783111342276-013_ref_041)\].
    
    ## 13.9 Conclusion
    
    In conclusion, a remarkable subject called QENN has emerged from the exciting junction of quantum computing and ML. This novel method offers a window into the future of computing and data analysis by attempting to take advantage of the special qualities of quantum algorithms to improve the performance of conventional neural networks. The research presented here highlights both the potential advantages and difficulties of QENN. Even while quantum computing holds great potential to transform many facets of ML, there are still numerous technological challenges to be addressed, including error correction and the development of useful quantum hardware. However, the theoretical foundation of this research creates intriguing opportunities for applications in domains including data analysis, pattern recognition, and optimization. The growing field of quantum technology is allowing academics and practitioners to leverage the potential of quantum computing to create more advanced and efficient neural network models, as the gap between quantum algorithms and ML gets smaller. QENN is a difficult concept to realize, but the potential benefits in terms of increased accuracy, quicker computing, and creative problem-solving strategies make the effort worthwhile.
    
    Future research in this burgeoning topic is probably going to result in the creation of useful QEML models, which will stimulate creativity and open up new avenues for innovation across a range of industries. Researchers can anticipate a time in the future when the marriage of ML and quantum algorithms will result in revolutionary developments in data science, AI, and computing technology as long as researchers can close the gap between these two domains.
